Zero-G achieves a $10\times$ latency improvement over existing strong decoders at matching accuracy, with worst-case sub-350ns decoding at code distances up to d=15, while scaling to 640 logical qubits on a single 128-core CPU and 32 logical qubits on a single AMD Versal V80 FPGA.

</details>


### [63] [Convergence monitoring of quantum Gibbs samplers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02038)
*Nikolaos Louloudis,Ruben Ibarrondo,Mikel Sanz,Mario Berta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a convergence monitoring criterion for quantum Gibbs samplers by analyzing energy-exchange statistics, leveraging weak measurements to enable efficient diagnostics with polynomial overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for efficient convergence diagnostics in quantum Gibbs-state sampling methods, particularly due to challenges in worst-case mixing time bounds for certain systems.

Method: The proposed criterion utilizes weak measurements and energy-exchange statistics to establish a Hamiltonian-agnostic stopping condition based on generated sample data.

Result: Statistical and numerical analyses demonstrate the effectiveness of the criterion in monitoring convergence, along with assessments of assumptions and limitations in various scenarios.

Conclusion: The approach offers a practical solution for efficiently diagnosing convergence in quantum Gibbs samplers, potentially accelerating the sampling process in quantum computations.

Abstract: Recent progress in fully quantum Markov chain Monte Carlo methods enables efficient Gibbs-state sampling on quantum computers [Chen et al., Nature 646, 561 (2025)]. Although rigorous worst-case bounds on mixing times remain largely inaccessible for classically intractable systems, experience from classical Monte Carlo suggests that convergence of relevant observables may nevertheless be rapid. This raises the practical question of how to diagnose convergence efficiently, i.e., with at most polynomial overhead. We propose a low-cost criterion for convergence monitoring that exploits the weak measurements inherent in quantum Gibbs samplers and their qubit-efficient variants [Ding et al., arXiv:2508.05703 (2025)]. Our approach is based on the observation that, at thermal equilibrium, the net energy flow between system and environment vanishes and energy-exchange statistics satisfy a balance condition. This condition appears in the distribution of (quasi-)frequencies extracted from the weak-measurement record and we use it to construct a Hamiltonian-agnostic stopping criterion based solely on data already generated by the sampler. We provide a statistical analysis, along with numerical and analytical studies to understand its performance, assumptions, and limitations.

</details>


### [64] [Towards Tensor-Network SAT-Solvers for Quantum-Classical Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02041)
*Benjamin Zec,Lukas Schmidbauer,Maja Franz,Wolfgang Mauerer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates integrated HPC/QC systems with a focus on surrogate selection in tensor-network optimization problems, revealing that encoding and instance awareness are critical for efficient architecture design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need to effectively integrate classical HPC with quantum computing, addressing complexity and optimizing performance through surrogate methods.

Method: The study involves comparing tensor-network approaches for optimization problems, notably Max-3-SAT, using both quantum and classical techniques, such as DMRG and simulated annealing.

Result: Findings indicate that transformations and surrogates significantly affect solution quality, with classical methods often matching or outperforming quantum techniques based on the problem instance.

Conclusion: Surrogate selection strategies for HPC/QC systems should be tailored to specific problem encodings and instances to enhance computational efficiency.

Abstract: Integrated HPC/QC systems aim to combine classical high-performance computing with quantum processors, but cannot be reduced to mechanisms for dispatching quantum kernels. An integrated architecture must support aspects such as observability, which cannot be implemented using QPUs alone, as well as fallback execution and cost-aware decisions on whether to replace quantum tasks with classical surrogates. Such mechanisms must be approximate or benefit from problem structure to soften the inescapable exponential classical worst-case complexity. In this work, we study tensor-network ground-state search, as such a surrogate, for optimisation problems. This combines key quantum primitives with advanced classical simulation. It provides initial empirical indicators for surrogate selection criteria, and exposes end-to-end toolchain effects that may be missed when transformation steps are studied in isolation. We compare a native polynomial unconstrained optimisation to-higher-order-Ising and a quadratised quadratic unconstrained binary optimization to-quadratic-Ising formulation for Max-3-SAT. Both are encoded as matrix product operator and optimised using density matrix renormalisation group approaches, with simulated annealing (SA) as classical performance baseline. Our results show that quadratisation is not a neutral transformation step: auxiliary variables and pairwise couplings substantially degrade solution quality relative to the native higher-order representation, while SA matches or outperforms DMRG across all tested instances. Since the optima of Boolean satisfiability (SAT)-derived problems are classical product states, DMRGs advantages dont materialise here. These findings suggest that surrogate selection in HPC/QC runtimes must be encoding- and instance-aware and provide empirical groundwork for informed decisions on fallback strategies and architecture co-design.

</details>


### [65] [Adaptive Reconstruction of Bosonic Quantum States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02049)
*Vasilisa Usova,Phila Rembold,Ian Yang,Marco Rossignolo,Simone Montangero,Samuele Tosatto,Gerhard Kirchmair*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an adaptive reconstruction technique for estimating fidelity in bosonic quantum systems, improving efficiency and robustness in measuring Wigner functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing challenges in characterizing bosonic quantum systems due to large Hilbert spaces and high tomography costs, especially in contexts where states differ by transformations.

Method: An adaptive reconstruction technique combining a physics-informed parametric model with Bayesian inference and active learning to iteratively select informative measurement points.

Result: The technique was implemented and benchmarked on Schrödinger cat states, yielding quick and reproducible fidelity estimates, and showing robustness to phase displacements and sensitivity to state imperfections.

Conclusion: The method enhances measurement efficiency for fidelity estimation in bosonic quantum states, proving beneficial for closed-loop quantum optimal control applications.

Abstract: Bosonic quantum systems provide a hardware-efficient platform for quantum information processing but remain challenging to characterise due to their large Hilbert space and the high measurement cost of state tomography. Existing approaches estimate the fidelity with respect to a single target state, making them unsuitable for applications in which physically equivalent states differ by phase space translations, rotations, or other transformations. Here, we introduce an adaptive reconstruction technique that estimates the fidelity with respect to a family of bosonic states while reconstructing the underlying Wigner function from a small number of measurements. The method combines a physics-informed parametric model with Bayesian inference, bootstrap, and active learning to iteratively select the most informative phase space sampling points. We implement the approach on a circuit quantum electrodynamics platform and benchmark it on Schrödinger cat states with amplitudes $α\in[1,3]$. The reconstruction yields reproducible fidelity estimates within a few minutes, remains robust to substantial displacements and rotations in phase space despite using a mismatched prior, and is sensitive to subtle state imperfections. We further compare the adaptive strategy with existing Wigner function sampling protocols experimentally, demonstrating the advantage of adaptive sampling for measurement-efficient fidelity estimation with respect to a family of cat states. Finally, we incorporate the reconstructed fidelity into the figure of merit used in a proof-of-principle closed-loop quantum optimal control experiment, demonstrating the applicability of the method to autonomous optimisation of bosonic quantum states.

</details>


### [66] [Scaled Caldeira-Leggett Dynamics: Deterministic Trajectories and the Classical Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02054)
*S. V. Mousavi,S. Miret-Artés*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper examines high-temperature dynamics in an open quantum system model using Bohmian mechanics, revealing that thermal effects result in deterministic trajectories even amid thermal noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the dynamics of open quantum systems in high-temperature regimes using a Bohmian perspective, aiming to understand the role of thermal effects in quantum-to-classical transitions.

Method: The authors express the density matrix in polar form to derive generalized continuity and Hamilton-Jacobi equations, incorporating an effective potential that retains nontrivial structure in the classical limit.

Result: The effective potential results in temperature-dependent trajectories that remain deterministic, contrasting with stochastic properties in Langevin dynamics, and revealing insights into coherence suppression due to thermal effects.

Conclusion: The findings highlight a significant interplay between thermal effects and quantum dynamics, demonstrating the deterministic nature of trajectories in a thermal environment and contributing to understanding quantum-to-classical transitions.

Abstract: The dynamics of an open quantum system in the high-temperature regime of the Caldeira-Leggett model using a Bohmian language is investigated. By expressing the density matrix in polar form, generalized continuity and Hamilton-Jacobi equations are obtained. Thermal effects appear explicitly in the former, while they influence the latter only indirectly through an effective potential. Remarkably, the effective potential does not vanish in the classical limit and cannot, in general, be decomposed into simple additive quantum and thermal contributions. Instead, it retains a nontrivial structure leading to a residual temperature dependence in the resulting dynamics. As a consequence, the resulting temperature-dependent-classical trajectories remain deterministic even in the presence of a thermal environment. This behavior contrasts with the stochastic dynamics observed in the Langevin description and reflects the ensemble-based nature of the present approach. To further explore the quantum-to-classical transition, a scaled version of the Caldeira-Leggett equation is introduced by scaling both the density matrix and Planck constant through a quantumness parameter. This parameter takes the value one in the fully quantum regime and smoothly approaches zero in the classical limit. Applications to Gaussian states demonstrate a gradual suppression of coherence and interference, governed jointly by thermal effects and the transition parameter.

</details>


### [67] [Characterizing the nitrogen-vacancy center singlet transition and its phonon sideband for absorption-based room-temperature magnetometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02060)
*Tobias Probst,Florian Schall,Sebastian Heuft,Janina J. Schindler,Lukas Lindner,Sven Mägdefessel,Rüdiger Quay,Philipp Reineck,Alexander M. Zaitsev,Takeshi Ohshima,Matthias Weidemüller,Jan Jeske*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates cavity-enhanced magnetometry using NV centers in diamond, focusing on optimizing absorption techniques to improve sensitivity and contrast for magnetic field detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance sensitivity and contrast in magnetometry using NV centers by employing absorption-based techniques within a cavity framework.

Method: The study employs cavity-enhanced spectroscopy and a novel method to measure room-temperature absorption cross sections, focusing on pump-laser and microwave-induced signal changes across a spectral range.

Result: The research identifies optimal contrast at 1042 nm and presents cavity-enhanced ODMR contrasts ranging from 50-80%. The absorption cross section at 1042 nm is quantified as $(0.89	ext{±}0.14) 	imes 10^{-21}	ext{m}^2$ or $(2.9	ext{±}0.5) 	imes 10^{-21}	ext{m}^2$ depending on reference parameters used.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates significant enhancement in absorption measurement techniques for NV centers, confirming multiple peaks of high contrast and providing precise values for the absorption cross sections.

Abstract: Magnetometry with nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond has shown great promise in recent years. In particular, absorption-based magnetometry techniques, employing a cavity to enhance the absorption length, can improve the contrast and sensitivity compared to conventional techniques based on reading out the NV$^-$ triplet fluorescence. The absorption techniques rely on magnetic-field-dependent absorption at the NV$^-$ singlet zero phonon line at 1042$\,$nm and its phonon sideband. In a cavity-enhanced spectroscopy approach, we study pump-laser- and microwave-induced cavity signal changes at room temperature over a spectral range of 680-1050$\,$nm. Through normalization, we eliminate the cavity-enhancement effect and provide quasi-single-pass values for the absorption and optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) contrast. The highest contrast is found at 1042$\,$nm, but multiple points of high contrast are found at the peaks of the phonon sideband. Additionally, cavity-enhanced ODMR contrasts in the range of 50-80$\,\%$ are presented. We further measure the broadband singlet absorption cross section at room temperature with a novel method through microwave-induced signal changes. This method is insensitive to pump-laser-induced signal changes by other defects and quantifies the room-temperature absorption strength of the singlet transition and its entire phonon sideband. We determine the absorption cross section at 1042$\,$nm to be $σ^{\,\bigstar}_{1042}=(0.89\pm0.14)\cdot 10^{-21}\,\text{m}^2$ or $σ^{\,\blacktriangle}_{1042}=(2.9\pm0.5)\cdot 10^{-21}\,\text{m}^2$. depending on the employed 532$\,$nm NV$^-$ absorption cross section.

</details>


### [68] [Efficient re-sampling in quasi-probability decompositions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02075)
*Sara Santos,Stefan Woerner,Vincenzo Savona,Julien Gacon*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work presents a reweighting strategy to reduce sampling overhead in quasi-probability decompositions (QPDs) for quantum circuits, enhancing practical use in variational algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-term quantum devices face limitations due to noise and hardware, prompting the need for algorithmic strategies that balance circuit complexity and sampling efficiency.

Method: The authors introduce a reweighting strategy for QPDs, enabling the reuse of samples across circuits with a common variational structure to mitigate exponential sampling overhead.

Result: The approach successfully estimates fidelities between parameterized quantum states and calculates the quantum geometric tensor, demonstrating superior performance under hardware noise compared to standard methods.

Conclusion: The findings suggest that reweighting strategies can significantly enhance the practicality of QPD-based methods in variational quantum algorithms.

Abstract: Near-term quantum devices are limited by noise and hardware constraints, motivating algorithmic approaches that trade circuit complexity for increased sampling overhead. Quasi-probability decompositions (QPDs), for example, allow replacing non-local operations by multiple circuits with local operations, but the associated sampling overhead generally scales exponentially and limits their practicality. In this work, we introduce a reweighting strategy for QPDs for circuits with the same variational structure across parameter settings, reusing samples and thereby reducing the sampling overhead. We first demonstrate this approach by estimating fidelities between parameterized quantum states, a key primitive in variational time evolution and quantum kernel methods. Importantly, this setup allows controlling the exponential QPD sampling overhead while preserving the structure of the state-encoding ansatz. We then apply the method to estimate the real part of the quantum geometric tensor using the simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation and find that, in the presence of realistic hardware noise, our method outperforms other standard estimation techniques. These results highlight the potential of reweighting strategies to extend the applicability of QPD-based methods in variational quantum algorithms.

</details>


### [69] [Adaptive Spectroscopy of Fast Two-Level-System Dynamics in Superconducting Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02086)
*Fabrizio Berritta,David Pahl,Lukas Pahl,William P. Banner,Gabriel Cutter,Jan A. Krzywda,Spencer Weeden,Shravan Patel,Paul Buttles,Stanislav Eilhart,Michael Gingras,Bethany M. Niedzielski,Robert McDermott,Mollie E. Schwartz,Kyle Serniak,Max Hays,Jeffrey A. Grover,William D. Oliver*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The study presents a novel adaptive spectroscopy method to observe fast dynamics of TLS defects in superconducting quantum processors, revealing time scales significantly shorter than conventional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parasitic TLS defects significantly impede the performance of superconducting quantum processors, causing energy relaxation and instability.

Method: The authors utilized sub-second adaptive spectroscopy and FPGA-based controllers to measure the switching dynamics of TLSs, achieving temporal and frequency resolution.

Result: They discovered that TLSs exhibit telegraphic switching with a timescale of seconds and spectral diffusion characterized by a diffusivity of about 0.9 MHz²/s, which is 300 times faster than traditional methods.

Conclusion: These findings uncover a new regime of TLS dynamics, which is critical for better characterization and calibration of superconducting quantum processors.

Abstract: Parasitic two-level-system (TLS) defects are a major source of energy relaxation and temporal instability in superconducting quantum processors. Our sub-second adaptive spectroscopy reveals telegraphic switching of TLSs with a characteristic timescale of a few seconds and spectral diffusion with diffusivity $D \approx 0.9~\mathrm{MHz}^2/\mathrm{s}$. These timescales are about $3 \times 10^2$ times faster than what is observed in conventional nonadaptive spectroscopy, which typically requires hours of measurement time. We resolve such fast dynamics on a field-programmable gate array (FPGA)-based controller that enables measurement of frequency- and time-resolved relaxations with sub-second temporal resolution in flux-tunable superconducting qubits. We observe similar defect dynamics across multiple qubits in independently fabricated devices measured in different laboratories. We correlate TLS-induced fluctuations with gate-level errors using randomized benchmarking. Our results reveal a previously inaccessible regime of frequency-resolved TLS dynamics and redefine the timescales relevant to TLS-aware characterization and calibration of superconducting quantum processors.

</details>


### [70] [Field-Free Transverse Aharonov--Bohm Phase Gate for an Orbital $l$-Qubit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02090)
*Ju Gao,Fang Shen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends the study of Aharonov–Bohm modes by examining the spin-resolved Dirac current in a confined geometry, demonstrating how confinement impacts the orbital phase and enables new qubit operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how confinement in a finite-wall annular guide affects the Aharonov–Bohm flux and the Dirac current, particularly in the context of qubit operations.

Method: The study employs theoretical modeling of a core-excluded finite-wall annular guide to analyze the spin-resolved Dirac current and its interactions under confinement.

Result: The confinement leads to a transverse Aharonov–Bohm propagation phase and allows the formation of same-path qubit states with a specific sensitivity and phase shift characteristics.

Conclusion: Finite-wall confinement converts the azimuthal Dirac current into a guided, field-free operation for generating phases, with potential applications in quantum computing.

Abstract: Free-space Aharonov--Bohm (AB) Bessel modes are known to carry a flux-dependent azimuthal Schrödinger probability current and kinetic orbital angular momentum. We extend this response to the spin-resolved conserved Dirac current of a core-excluded finite-wall annular guide and show that confinement converts its surviving orbital component into a transverse Aharonov--Bohm (TAB) propagation phase for a straight traveling mode whose centroid path has zero projection onto $\mathbf A$. The radial-gradient current reverses under spin reversal; retaining the complete evanescent tail closes it as a boundary contribution, leaving a spin-independent orbital phase $Δφ_{ln}\propto lΦL_{\rm int}\langleρ^{-2}\rangle_{ln}/v_z$. Opposite-winding modes $|\pm l\rangle$ form a same-path qubit implementing $R_z(2δ_l)$ with differential readout and common-mode phase rejection, while direct first-order crosstalk requires the angular harmonic $m=\pm2l$. For a $100\,μ\mathrm{m}$ section with $a=20\,\mathrm{nm}$, $R=30\,\mathrm{nm}$, $E_z=10\,\mathrm{meV}$, and $|l|=10$, the sensitivity is $0.1885\,\mathrm{mrad/mG}$ and $R_z(π)$ occurs at $16.67\,\mathrm{G}$. Finite-wall confinement thus turns intrinsic azimuthal Dirac current into a guided field-free phase operation.

</details>


### [71] [Better accuracy with fewer qubits: Single-particle basis set optimization for quantum chemistry on quantum computers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02119)
*Subimal Deb,V. S. Prasannaa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents modified minimal basis sets (MSTO-kG) that enhance quantum chemical calculations on noisy quantum computers by achieving comparable or superior energies to existing basis sets while using fewer qubits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitation of noisy quantum computers, which restrict quantum chemical calculations to small active spaces, leading to loss of correlation energy.

Method: The authors utilize a genetic algorithm-inspired approach to optimize existing minimal basis sets, creating the MSTO-kG bases for atoms H through F and evaluating their performance against standard basis sets.

Result: The MSTO bases show improved or comparable atomic energies relative to 6-31G and cc-pVQZ bases, with better resource efficiency in terms of qubit count and gate operations across various methods.

Conclusion: MSTO bases offer a path to more accurate quantum chemical calculations with fewer qubit requirements, making them suitable for near-term quantum computers.

Abstract: In spite of recent advances, quantum computers are expected to be sufficiently noisy in the coming few years to the extent of limiting quantum chemical calculations to relatively small number of orbitals. However, even with reasonable quality single particle basis sets, small active spaces with limited orbitals can result in a significant fraction of correlation energy being lost, motivating the design of moderate quality qubit-efficient basis sets for quantum algorithms. We begin by reoptimizing the existing minimal basis sets using a genetic algorithm-inspired approach in conjunction with aggressive refinement strategies, and generate modified minimal basis sets (MSTO-kG basis; k = 2-11) for atoms from H through F. The ground state energies of H through F using our MSTO bases at FCI level of theory yield ground state energies that are comparable or sometimes even lower than those obtained using 6-31G basis sets. In the case of Li, the MSTO bases surpass the performance of cc-pVQZ bases. Thus, we obtain better atomic energies with same number of qubits relative to STO bases, and better/comparable energies with fewer qubits relative to higher quality bases. In the case of molecules, H2 performs poorly; a finding that is consistent with an earlier work in literature. For other molecules, Li2, C2, LiH, BeH and BeH2, the FCI results (except C2 for which we employ CISD) from our bases are comparable to/outperform those from 6-31G basis. Finally, we compare the resources required between different bases and find that MSTO bases yield better energies than the competing basis sets while incurring fewer qubits and two-qubit gates with VQE, QPE, and HHL. The logical T-gate counts are also found to be considerably lower for QPE and HHL respectively. Overall, our work paves way for more accurate yet less qubit-hungry quantum chemical calculations using near-term quantum computers.

</details>


### [72] [Unconditionally successful quantum Time-Marching algorithm via LCU for nonlinear Burgers equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02130)
*Niccolo Fonio,Giuseppe Di Molfetta,Pierre Sagaut*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a quantum algorithm for Burgers equation that achieves unconditional success without the need for postselection, leveraging quantum lattice gas principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitations of existing quantum algorithms for partial differential equations that rely on non-unitary operations and probabilistic postselection, which increase computational costs.

Method: The paper presents quantum lattice gas algorithms that enable unconditionally successful simulations of nonlinearities, particularly focusing on Burgers equation.

Result: The proposed quantum algorithm allows for concatenation of time steps without probabilistic failure, demonstrating success in simulating nonlinear equations.

Conclusion: This work identifies properties of classical probabilistic algorithms that can be applied to this formulation, broadening the scope of effective quantum simulations.

Abstract: Most recently proposed quantum algorithms for solving linear and nonlinear partial differential equations rely on non-unitary operations. These operations are typically implemented probabilistically, requiring postselection and thus increasing the computational cost. We show that quantum lattice gas algorithms enable unconditionally successful quantum simulation of nonlinearities, yielding, to our knowledge, the first quantum algorithm for Burgers equation whose time steps can be concatenated without probabilistic failure. The key idea is to exploit the correspondence between the stochasticity of quantum measurement in the linear combination of unitaries framework and the intrinsic randomness of the classical lattice gas algorithm. In doing so, we identify general properties that characterize probabilistic classical algorithms amenable to this time-marching formulation, and illustrate the approach with an additional application.

</details>


### [73] [A Standard Quantum Mechanical Treatment to Rationalize the Delayed-Choice Quantum Eraser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02185)
*Vipul Badhan,Pranvi,Samreet Dhillon,Bindiya Arora*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper clarifies the foundations of delayed-choice quantum erasure experiments, illustrating that erasure and which-path decisions do not alter a photon's history, and demonstrating this through standard quantum mechanics frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the foundational paradoxes in delayed-choice quantum erasure experiments and provide a clearer understanding of quantum measurement implications.

Method: Utilizing an extended Mach-Zehnder interferometer to analyze measurement bases, and mapping the experiment to a two-way Stern-Gerlach framework to demonstrate quantum erasure as a result of entangled state measurements.

Result: The study shows that the choice between which-path and erasure detectors is merely a measurement basis choice, not a rewrite of history, and confirms quantum erasure as expected for entangled states.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that perceived retrocausality results from improper questioning, and a standard forward-in-time interpretation suffices for understanding these quantum experiments.

Abstract: Ever since the proposal of delayed choice quantum erasure and subsequent realization in the experiment by Kim et al., the interpretation and implications of delayed-choice experiments have remained a subject of intense foundational debate. This paper resolves the apparent paradox attached to the experiment using standard quantum mechanics. Using an extended Mach-Zehnder interferometer which captures every operational feature of the original experiment, we show that choosing between which-path and erasure detectors is simply a choice of measurement bases, which does not rewrite a photon's past. Furthermore, by mapping the experiment to a two-way Stern-Gerlach framework, we prove that quantum erasure is an expected result of measuring entangled states, not a physical anomaly. Ultimately, through a pedagogical game, we illustrate that the illusion of retrocausality arises from asking illegitimate questions, and that a forward-in-time description is entirely sufficient to explain the logic.

</details>


### [74] [Quantum computer-based simulation of Stark many-body localization in a 1D Fermi-Hubbard model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02245)
*Abdul Kalam,Prasenjit Deb,Akitada Sakurai,Tapan Mishra,V. S. Prasannaa,B. P. Das*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work investigates many-body localization (MBL) in a quantum system, illustrating its dynamics through simulations on a quantum computer while optimizing their execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study of MBL is crucial for understanding non-ergodicity and entanglement behavior in quantum systems, particularly in light of potential applications in quantum computing.

Method: The authors employed Hamiltonian simulation on a 12-qubit Fermi-Hubbard model using IBM superconducting qubit hardware, integrating advanced compilation techniques and circuit optimizations to reduce computational overhead.

Result: The implementation resulted in significant reductions of approximately 88% in two-qubit gate count and 87% in circuit depth, enabling efficient simulation of dynamic behaviors in MBL.

Conclusion: The simulations demonstrated the transition from thermalizing to strongly localized dynamics, supported by benchmarking against exact results, showcasing the potential of using quantum hardware for MBL studies.

Abstract: Many-body localization (MBL) is a dynamical phenomenon that describes the non-ergodicity of isolated quantum many-body systems. In contrast to thermalization, this phenomenon leads to a long-lived memory of initial states of local systems and slow growth of entanglement. In this work, we study Stark MBL in a 12-qubit correlated fermionic system described by the one-dimensional Fermi-Hubbard model using Hamiltonian simulation on an IBM superconducting qubit quantum computer. To enable such a computation on current-day noisy hardware, we combine a series of compilation steps, including the use of the spin-resolved Jordan-Wigner transformation, employing SWAP networks, and integrating a tensor-network-based quantum circuit optimization routine on top of a standard circuit optimization pipeline. As a result, there is approximately an 88$\%$ and 87$\%$ reduction in two-qubit gate count and circuit depth, respectively. Through such simulations of the real-time dynamics using Trotterized quantum circuits, we exhibit a crossover from thermalizing dynamics of the system at a weak tilt of the field to a strongly localized behavior at large tilt with short evolution times. We also benchmark our obtained results with respect to those from exact simulations.

</details>


### [75] [Effects of realistic pulse shapes in two-dimensional spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02275)
*M. Russo,R. Gilliot,A. Blech,M. Joffre,C. P. Koch,H. Seiler,Département de Physique,Institut Polytechnique de Paris,Palaiseau,France,Laboratoire d'Optique et Biosciences,CNRS,Inserm,École Polytechnique,Institut Polytechnique de Paris,Palaiseau,France)*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient method for simulating 2D spectroscopy to analyze the effects of non-Gaussian pulse shapes and spectral phase distortions on optical responses in quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the accuracy and efficiency of 2D spectroscopy simulations, especially in considering realistic experimental conditions.

Method: An efficient simulation method in the Markovian limit that accommodates arbitrary pulse shapes and examines time-ordering and overlapping pulse effects with linear computational cost relative to sampling points.

Result: Realistic pulse shapes induce additional spectral features and dynamics that can lead to observable changes in 2D spectra, highlighting the impact of spectral phase distortions and the necessity for accurate modeling.

Conclusion: Incorporating realistic pulses in simulations is crucial for accurate interpretation of 2D spectra and understanding pulse-induced effects in quantum systems.

Abstract: Two-dimensional (2D) spectroscopy is a powerful pump-pump-probe technique for revealing couplings between quantum states and disentangling the different contributions to the optical response of a system. We present an efficient method for 2D spectroscopy simulations in the Markovian limit for the environment, capable of handling arbitrary pulse shapes and reproducing time-ordering and overlapping pulse effects, while maintaining a computational cost that scales linearly with the number of sampling points. We leverage this framework to investigate how 2D spectra are affected by spectral phase distortions and highly non-Gaussian pulse shapes, such as those produced experimentally by hollow-core fibers or non-collinear optical amplifiers. We show that realistic pulses can induce the appearance of additional spectral features, lineshape distortions and oscillating contributions in the system's dynamics. Notably, even weak temporal pulse tails arising from uncorrected high-order spectral phase terms cause visible changes in the 2D spectra. We also find that homodyne detection schemes employed in experiments can mitigate the presence of such pulse effects. These results emphasize the importance of including realistic pulses in 2D spectroscopy simulations to identify pulse-induced effects and minimize ambiguities in the interpretation of experimental data.

</details>


### [76] [Learnable yet not simulable: a quantum resource theory of learning models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02325)
*Xinbiao Wang,Yuxuan Du,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates the role of quantum resources in the classical learnability of quantum circuits, introducing a new measure called dynamical stabilizer entropy (DSE) that aids in understanding this relationship, ultimately defining a computational phase diagram for simulators and establishing boundaries for efficient learning in quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify which quantum resource determines the classical learnability of quantum circuits outside the realm of efficient classical simulation.

Method: The authors introduce the dynamical stabilizer entropy (DSE) as a new resource measure, relate it to operator stabilizer entropy, and construct a computational phase diagram comparing classical simulators and quantum-data-assisted surrogates. They derive bounds on sample complexity and runtime for classical surrogates, guided by DSE.

Result: Numerical experiments reveal a DSE-dependent learnability boundary, supporting the establishment of the computational phase diagram and the efficient learnability of certain circuit families by the DSE-guided surrogate.

Conclusion: The findings provide a quantitative framework for distinguishing between classical simulation and learning, suggesting future directions for learning-based algorithms applied to scalable quantum systems.

Abstract: Quantum resource theory has sharpened our understanding of the intrinsic complexity of quantum systems, particularly their classical simulability. However, it remains unclear which quantum resource governs the classical learnability of quantum circuits, especially beyond the regime of efficient classical simulation. Here we close this knowledge gap by studying the expectation-value functions of families of tunable quantum circuits, with many applications in digital quantum simulation, quantum metrology, and quantum-system characterization. Specifically, we introduce a new resource measure, the dynamical stabilizer entropy (\DSE), which quantifies how broadly an expectation-value function is distributed across its frequency modes. By relating \DSE to operator stabilizer entropy, we establish a computational phase diagram that compares classical simulators with quantum-data-assisted classical surrogates. We first determine the \DSE-dependent learnability boundary of this diagram by deriving bounds on the sample complexity and runtime of classical surrogates, and by developing a \DSE-guided surrogate. We then complete the diagram by proving, under standard complexity-theoretic assumptions, the existence of circuit families that can be efficiently learned by this surrogate but cannot be efficiently emulated from their circuit descriptions alone. Numerical experiments on random and structured circuits with up to 80 qubits support the predicted \DSE-dependent computational landscape. These results establish a quantitative resource-theoretic framework for delineating the boundary between classical simulation and learning, motivate resource measures linking quantum resources to learnability, and guide the design of learning-based algorithms for scalable quantum systems beyond the reach of direct classical simulation.

</details>


### [77] [Finite-Syndrome Compression of Quantum Fisher Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02333)
*Jianqi Sheng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a framework for optimizing quantum error correction by analyzing the relationship between finite syndrome values and the loss of quantum Fisher information, introducing a novel way to design optimal error records while maintaining metrological information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in knowledge about how to merge finite error records without compromising quantum metrology, particularly in the context of noise trajectories in quantum sensing.

Method: The authors derive an identity that links the loss of symmetric-logarithmic-derivative quantum Fisher information to distances between error scores, framing the problem within operator-valued clustering for optimal syndrome design.

Result: The paper establishes a precise link between the number of readable syndromes and the chromatic number of a specific graph, providing finite partition formulas for quantum noise models, and demonstrating the conditions for zero information loss in syndrome merging.

Conclusion: The findings elucidate the information-theoretic trade-offs in quantum error correction, contributing to the understanding of how finite syndrome resolution affects quantum state recovery and noise management.

Abstract: Readable error records can protect quantum sensing because they prevent physically distinct noise trajectories from being irreversibly mixed. A finite detector or ancilla, however, can retain only finitely many syndrome values, and a general criterion for deciding which records may be merged without losing metrological information is absent. We formulate the problem for a fixed fine-grained classical-quantum record and parameter-independent compression into at most $M$ flags. We prove an exact identity expressing the lost symmetric-logarithmic-derivative quantum Fisher information (SLD QFI) as a sum of state-weighted squared distances between fine and coarse SLD scores. Consequently, optimal finite-syndrome design is exactly an operator-valued clustering problem, and zero loss is characterized by a support-resolved common-SLD condition. We extend the identity to the full multiparameter SLD QFI matrix and distinguish local QFI preservation from recovery of an entire statistical model. For exact recovery of a quantum code, we separately show that, when each fine error is individually correctable, the minimum number of readable syndromes is the chromatic number of a Knill-Laflamme incompatibility graph. For qubit random-unitary noise we obtain a finite partition formula. A planar random-Pauli model admits a conditional-variance representation and, for a uniform error axis, the exact optimum $F_M^{\star}=[M\sin(π/M)/π]^2$, with deficit $π^2/(3M^2)+O(M^{-4})$. These results identify the information-theoretic cost of finite syndrome resolution while making explicit the side-information assumptions required for any passive noise-to-erasure interpretation.

</details>


### [78] [Revealing and reducing growth-induced interfacial disorder in preferentially aligned nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02350)
*Cheng-I Ho,Marina Davydova,Patrik Straňák,Felix Hoffmann,Peter Knittel,Andrej Denisenko,Jörg Wrachtrup*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates the effects of nitrogen injection methods on the quality of nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, revealing that smooth nitrogen delivery minimizes defects and enhances coherence properties for quantum sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the performance of NV centers in CVD diamond thin films for imaging and sensing applications by addressing interfacial disorder caused by nitrogen injection.

Method: Two nitrogen-injection methods (pulsed and smooth delivery) were employed on $^{12}	ext{C}$-enriched NV layers, followed by selective plasma etching and NV spin-coherence measurements to assess the quality of the films.

Result: Pulsed injection led to excessive defects, while smooth delivery significantly reduced interfacial disorder, achieving coherence properties near theoretical limits and demonstrating effective proton nuclear magnetic resonance detection with a 50-nm NV layer.

Conclusion: The study highlights the importance of nitrogen-doping methods in minimizing interfacial disorder, paving the way for high-quality NV-doped layers suitable for advanced quantum sensing.

Abstract: Nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in chemical-vapor-deposition (CVD) diamond can form preferentially oriented ensembles with high sensing performance and low densities of lattice defects. Thin films of this material are a cornerstone of various imaging modalities. However, nitrogen injection needed to produce such films can transiently drive growth out of equilibrium, generating interfacial strain and spin defects that degrade NV coherence. Here, we investigate this disorder in $^{12}\text{C}$-enriched, preferentially oriented NV layers grown on (111) diamond using two nitrogen-injection procedures, combined with nanometer-scale selective plasma etching and NV spin-coherence measurements. Pulsed nitrogen injection produces a pronounced nitrogen overshoot within a 60--80 nm interfacial region, generating excessive amounts of defects. By contrast, smooth nitrogen delivery through mass flow controllers substantially suppresses interfacial disorder, yielding coherence properties close to the theoretical limit imposed by spin-bath noise. A 50-nm NV layer is used to demonstrate proton nuclear magnetic resonance detection. This work reveals the role of interfacial disorder associated with the nitrogen-doping procedure and provides a route to growing high-quality, thin NV-doped layers for quantum-sensing applications.

</details>


### [79] [Entanglement Generation Beyond Quantum Theory: From Product States to Popescu-Rohrlich Boxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02403)
*Shun Umekawa,Akihiro Hokkyo,Hayato Arai,Kazuaki Takasan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper reveals a method for entanglement generation in boxworld by relaxing reversibility to pure-state preservation, identifying transformations that map uncorrelated pure states to Popescu--Rohrlich boxes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the generation of entanglement in boxworld, a framework that allows for supraquantum correlations absent in quantum mechanics, and to understand the role of reversibility.

Method: A transformation is constructed that preserves pure states while mapping uncorrelated ones to Popescu--Rohrlich boxes, along with a complete classification of such transformations in bipartite boxworld.

Result: The study provides a mechanism for generating beyond-quantum entanglement without mixing, in contrast to quantum descriptions.

Conclusion: The findings highlight a key distinction between reversibility and pure-state preservation, offering new insights into the dynamics of entanglement generation in non-quantum frameworks.

Abstract: Entanglement generation is a fundamental dynamical capability in quantum information science and underpins many quantum advantages. While quantum theory enables it through unitary dynamics, boxworld, a generalized probabilistic theory admitting Popescu--Rohrlich boxes with supraquantum correlations, has no reversible transformation capable of generating entanglement. We show that this no-go picture changes fundamentally once reversibility is relaxed to pure-state preservation. We construct a pure-state-preserving transformation that maps every uncorrelated pure state to a Popescu--Rohrlich box and completely classify all pure-state-preserving entangling transformations in the simplest bipartite boxworld. Our results provide the first explicit mechanism for generating beyond-quantum entanglement without introducing mixing and demonstrate a physical distinction between reversibility and pure-state preservation that is obscured by the structure of quantum theory.

</details>


### [80] [Caustics and Superenergy in the Quantum Bouncer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02427)
*Marko. M. Ćosić,Andrew N. Jordan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores quantum caustics in the quantum bouncing ball problem, revealing relationships between classical trajectories, phase singularity chains, and superenergy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the relationship between classical caustics and quantum phenomena, focusing on quantum interference in the bouncing ball problem.

Method: Analyzed a Gaussian wavepacket while connecting phase singularities to the semiclassical Pearcey function and quantified superenergy in the context of Bohm trajectories.

Result: Demonstrated the emergence of caustics aligned with classical trajectories and identified regions of superenergy exceeding the energy eigenvalue.

Conclusion: Provided a comprehensive understanding of quantum caustics through both phase singularities and trajectory analysis, bridging classical and quantum mechanics.

Abstract: We investigate the quantum interference and energetic phenomena associated with classical caustics in the quantum bouncing ball problem, we refer to as quantum caustics. By considering an initial Gaussian wavepacket, we show that caustics associated with the underlying classical trajectory families are exhibited. We connect the associated phase singularity chains in the vicinity of the caustic with the semiclassical Pearcey function built on the cusp catastrophe lines. We also quantify the amount of superenergy exhibited in these solutions - regions of space where the local energy exceeds the largest constituent energy eigenvalue. We give a complimentary description of the caustic and superenergy behavior using the Madelung/Bohm trajectories, which gives additional insight about the energy of the trajectories and how they traverse the phase singularity chains.

</details>


### [81] [Detecting high-dimensional entanglement with simple measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02439)
*Suraj Goel,Alexander Bernal,Gabriele Cobucci,Will McCutcheon,Mehul Malik,Armin Tavakoli*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a simplified scheme for detecting Schmidt numbers, crucial for assessing high-dimensional entanglement, using only single-qubit measurements, thus reducing complexity and implementation costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To make the detection of high-dimensional entanglement more accessible and less costly as quantum technology advances.

Method: The study develops a measurement scheme relying solely on sequences of single-qubit observables, utilizing low-depth quantum circuits for detection.

Result: The method successfully identifies Schmidt numbers in up to sixteen-dimensional photonic entanglement, demonstrating a significant reduction in experimental setup complexity.

Conclusion: Simpler and more scalable measurements are effective for detecting high-dimensional entanglement properties, allowing broader applications in quantum technology.

Abstract: The standard benchmark for high-dimensional entanglement is the number of dimensions in which entanglement must be present in order to generate the state. This is called the Schmidt number and its detection is usually based on implementing an appropriate set of local basis measurements. However, as quantum technology brings increasingly large physical dimensions within reach, the implementation of such measurements typically becomes more costly. Here, we develop a scheme for detecting Schmidt numbers based only on sequences of single-qubit observables. These measurements are simpler to implement as they require only low-depth quantum circuits. Using up to sixteen-dimensional photonic spatial mode entanglement and multi-plane light conversion technology, we demonstrate how it simplifies setup complexity and successfully detects the maximal (or close-to-maximal) Schmidt number. Our results reveal that simple and more scalable measurements are sufficient to detect high-dimensional entanglement properties.

</details>


### [82] [Spread complexity as a probe in generalized and long-range Aubry-Andre-Harper models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02451)
*Triyas Sapui,Tanoy Kanti Konar,Subinay Dasgupta,Aditi Sen De*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study examines quantum quench dynamics in generalized long-range Aubry-Andre-Harper models, revealing nonanalytic behavior of spread complexity at mobility edge transitions and deriving key analytical expressions for moments and Lanczos coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the spread complexity and mobility edges in quantum quenches is crucial for insights into quantum state dynamics in various physical systems.

Method: Investigation of spread complexity and local density of states in generalized AAH models, with analytical derivations of moments and Lanczos coefficients under different quasiperiodic potential strengths.

Result: Long-time averaged spread complexity shows nonanalytic behavior as quasiperiodic potential crosses the mobility edge. Distinct behaviors of Lanczos coefficients depending on the presence or absence of mobility edges were identified.

Conclusion: The results elucidate the impact of mobility edges on quantum dynamics, highlighting significant differences in behavior based on the nature of the quench.

Abstract: We investigate the spread complexity of quantum quenches in generalized and long-range Aubry-Andre-Harper (AAH) models, encompassing regimes with and without mobility edges. In particular, in the generalized AAH models supporting energy-dependent mobility edges, we demonstrate that the long-time averaged spread complexity exhibits nonanalytic behavior when the post-quench quasiperiodic potential crosses the mobility edge associated with the energy of the initial eigenstate, thereby accurately identifying the mobility-edge transition. Such a behavior is supported by the spreading of local density of states. We further derive analytical expressions for the moments and the corresponding Lanczos coefficients for quenches between the limits of vanishing and strong quasiperiodic potentials. The Lanczos coefficients display qualitatively distinct behavior depending on the presence of mobility edges - they exhibit an initial plateau followed by a decay with the Krylov basis index, in contrast to the nearly constant behavior of the conventional AAH model without mobility edges. For LR hopping, the coefficients decay with the Krylov basis index for quenches from the localized to the extended phase, while they coincide with the AAH results for quenches in the opposite direction.

</details>


### [83] [Nanohertz Pendulum toward Macroscopic Entanglement under Structural Damping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02462)
*Azusa Sawada,Hina Nakano,Kanta Watanabe,Gaku Ohashi,Shota Okumura,Nobuyuki Matsumoto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper discusses optimizing a pendulum design for macroscopic quantum control by addressing structural noise and improving gain to exceed requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pendulums are ideal for macroscopic quantum control due to gravity dilution and potential for low mechanical loss, but low-frequency noise can hinder entanglement.

Method: A 7-mg pendulum was suspended using a stepped fused-silica fiber, achieving a low energy-decay rate and measuring gain significantly above the requirement despite detecting losses.

Result: With 10% detection loss, the pendulum shows a required suspension gain of 1.49 and a measured gain of approximately 2.5, which exceeds the threshold needed to mitigate structural noise.

Conclusion: This design successfully overcomes structural-noise penalties, enhancing entanglement capability for quantum control applications.

Abstract: Pendulums are attractive for macroscopic quantum control because gravity dilution reduces mechanical loss, while the $1/f$ force-noise spectrum associated with structural damping allows nearly lossless trapping to suppress the thermal noise sampled at an upward-shifted resonance. The same $1/f$ spectrum, however, produces a low-frequency tail that penalizes entanglement. With $10\%$ detection loss, we find that this tail raises the required back-action-to-thermal force-noise ratio by about $50\%$, corresponding to a required suspension gain $G_{\rm req}=1.49$. To overcome this structural-noise penalty, we realize a $7$-mg pendulum suspended by a stepped fused-silica fiber, with an energy-decay rate $Γ/2π=361(39)$ nHz ($Q\equivω_0/Γ=7.3(8)\times10^6$) at $ω_0/2π=2.63$ Hz. The reduction in $ω_0Γ$ yields a measured gain $G_q\simeq2.5$ relative to the previous monolithic device, exceeding the requirement.

</details>


### [84] [High-dimensional quantum process tomography with undetected photons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02490)
*Salini Rajeev,Mayukh Lahiri*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a novel interferometric method for quantum process tomography that allows full reconstruction of quantum operations on high-dimensional states (qudits) without measurement on the transformed state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of existing quantum process tomography methods that require measurement on transformed qudits.

Method: An interferometric approach to reconstruct arbitrary operations on high-dimensional quantum states without the necessity of measurement.

Result: The proposed method enables complete characterization of quantum operations on qudits where traditional measurements are not required.

Conclusion: This novel technique broadens the possibilities for quantum state characterization, facilitating more efficient process tomography.

Abstract: The goal of quantum process tomography is to fully characterize an operation performed on a quantum state. By considering high-dimensional quantum states (qudit), we show that it is possible to fully reconstruct an arbitrary operation without performing any measurement on the transformed qudit. Our method is interferometric and conceptually different from existing techniques of quantum process tomography that must perform a measurement on the transformed qudit.

</details>


### [85] [Thermalization of open quantum systems with pseudomodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02517)
*R. Kevin Kessing,Thibaut Lacroix,Susana F. Huelga,Martin B. Plenio*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates the thermalization behavior of quantum systems in pseudomode approaches, focusing on conditions for satisfying detailed balance and the implications for thermodynamic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the thermalization of quantum systems interacting with baths under varying coupling conditions, particularly when pseudomode models are employed.

Method: The study examines the conditions under which pseudomode models exhibit detailed balance and explores parameter choices that allow for 'weak' detailed balance within specific frequency ranges.

Result: Findings indicate that pseudomode models can fail to reach the Gibbs state even with asymptotically weak coupling, and establish guidelines for achieving thermalization and detailed balance in these systems.

Conclusion: The results provide essential insights for designing pseudomode models with thermodynamic consistency, ensuring correct thermal behavior of quantum systems.

Abstract: Pseudomode approaches allow for an exact and unapproximated description of a quantum system interacting arbitrarily strongly with a bath. In general, a system coupled to pseudomodes will not thermalize to the system's Gibbs state: This is to be expected when the system-bath coupling is non-perturbative, but conflicts with common thermodynamic intuition when the system-bath coupling is asymptotically weak. We explore under which circumstances pseudomode models satisfy detailed balance (and subsequently thermalize to the system Gibbs state) and how specific choices of parameters can force "weak" detailed balance that is restricted to a limited frequency range. A combination of Hermitian and non-Hermitian pseudomodes that yields a flat effective-temperature profile is also considered. The results and criteria established here are relevant for the construction of pseudomode models in contexts where thermodynamic consistency is required.

</details>


### [86] [Squeezing-Fueled Quantum Otto Engine via Measurement-Induced Cooling: The Two-Qubit Quantum Rabi Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02521)
*S R Rathnakaran,Asoka Biswas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study explores a quantum Otto engine utilizing two qubits and a non-Markovian thermal bath, highlighting the benefits of cavity squeezing on efficiency and power output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the performance improvement of quantum Otto engines using innovative cooling methods and squeezing mechanisms in cavity QED systems.

Method: The engine utilizes a two-qubit quantum Rabi model, with a single non-Markovian thermal bath. Cooling is achieved through projective measurement on the cavity mode, while a squeezing drive acts as a quantum fuel.

Result: Cavity squeezing enhances both power output and operational efficiency, exceeding the standard quantum Otto limit, with efficiency converging to the Otto bound from above in the limit-cycle regime.

Conclusion: Squeezing is identified as a key quantum resource for optimizing thermodynamic performance in novel multi-resource quantum heat engines.

Abstract: We investigate a quantum Otto engine (QOE) constructed from the two-qubit quantum Rabi model, operating within a cavity quantum electrodynamics (QED) architecture. The engine operates with two qubits as the working substance and a single non-Markovian hot thermal bath, modeled via the hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) formalism. In place of a conventional cold thermal reservoir, the cooling stroke is realized through a projective measurement protocol on the cavity mode, which acts as an ancillary subsystem and effectively mimics a cold bath for the qubit working medium via measurement back-action. A squeezing drive applied to the cavity mode serves as a quantum fuel. We demonstrate that cavity squeezing systematically enhances both the power output and operational efficiency of the engine - the work extracted per unit of heat drawn from the hot bath-driving it above the standard quantum Otto limit. In the limit-cycle regime, the efficiency, while remaining above the Otto bound throughout, asymptotically converges to it from above. This identifies squeezing as a controllable quantum resource for thermodynamic optimization. Our results reveal that the interplay between qubit-cavity coupling, measurement-induced cooling, and non-equilibrium squeezing gives rise to a multi-resource thermodynamic architecture with performance characteristics inaccessible to conventional two-bath quantum Otto engines, thereby providing a concrete route toward experimentally realizable quantum heat engines in cavity QED platforms.

</details>


### [87] [Entanglement of flower states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02587)
*Samrat Sen,Ludovico Lami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the operational structure of entanglement using 'flower states' and reveals a notable gap between distillable entanglement and entanglement cost under local operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the operational characteristics of entanglement through the lens of flower states and to analyze the differences in entanglement measures using these states.

Method: The authors compute various entanglement measures for flower states, examining distillable entanglement and the entanglement cost under local operations and classical communication (LOCC), as well as non-entangling operations (NE). They calculate tempered entanglement negativity and analyze its implications.

Result: The paper finds a significant gap in entanglement measures: distillable entanglement remains constant at 1 ebit while entanglement cost varies with local dimension, revealing the largest known irreversibility gap under NE operations and demonstrating that squashed entanglement is not a monotone under NE operations.

Conclusion: The exact entanglement cost under LOCC for flower states is equal to the Schmidt number, which is additive over multiple copies, and shows that the complexity of entanglement manipulation depends on the local dimension.

Abstract: The mysterious nature of entanglement, one of the most prominent exquisitely quantum phenomena, is reflected in its intricate operational structure, with a hierarchy of classes of free operations that enable its manipulation at different levels of effectiveness. Here we use the class of 'flower states', parametrised by their (even) local dimension $2k$, to shine light on some aspects of this varied landscape. We compute all the main entanglement measures for flower states, uncovering a large gap between all forms of distillable entanglement, equal to 1 ebit independently of the local dimension, and the entanglement cost under local operations and classical communication (LOCC), known to be equal to $\log\big(2\sqrt{k}\big)$. Even under the strictly more powerful class of non-entangling (NE) operations, we show that their cost is still equal to $\log\big(1+\sqrt{k}\big)$, only about an ebit less than for LOCCs. This result, which we prove by calculating the recently introduced tempered entanglement negativity for these states, demonstrates the largest known 'irreversibility gap', i.e. the difference between distillable entanglement and entanglement cost, under NE operations, equal to $Θ\big(\frac12 \log d\big)$, with $d$ being the local dimension. A notable consequence is that the celebrated squashed entanglement is not a monotone under NE operations. Finally, we compute the exact cost under LOCC operations for flower states; this is given by the Schmidt number, which turns out to be additive over multiple copies and equal to $\min_{r|k} \log\left( r + \frac{k}{r} \right)$; for prime $k$ this reduces to $\log(k+1)$, about twice the standard LOCC cost. These last results leverage the uncertainty relations over cyclic groups proved by Tao and Meshulam.

</details>


### [88] [Optimal Quantum de Finetti Theorems via Argmax Rounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02590)
*Fernando Granha Jeronimo,Pei Wu,Haochen Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Optimal finite quantum de Finetti bounds are proven for bosonic states, showing dimension-related results and applications in quantum channels and algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address dimension dependence in quantum de Finetti bounds and to settle questions left open by prior research.

Method: Using a proof based on sum-of-squares rounding, the argmax method, and formulating de Finetti approximation as the integrality gap of a symmetric-extension semidefinite program.

Result: Achieved optimal bounds for bosonic states and established results for arbitrary exchangeable states and applications in channels and algorithms.

Conclusion: Introduced a dimension-free bosonic de Finetti theorem and provided implications for separability and computational complexity.

Abstract: We prove optimal finite quantum de Finetti upper bounds. Given a bosonic state $ρ_N\in D(\mathrm{Sym}^N(\mathbb C^d))$, there is a probability measure $ν$ on the unit sphere such that \[
  \left\|
  ρ_N^{(2)}-\int |u\rangle\langle u|^{\otimes 2}\,dν(u)
  \right\|_1
  \le \frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}. \] By purification, the bosonic theorem also gives the optimal $O(d/N)$ upper bound for arbitrary exchangeable states. These results settle the dimension dependence left open by Christandl, König, Mitchison, and Renner (CMP 2007). The proof casts de Finetti approximation as sum-of-squares rounding and applies the argmax method of Jeronimo, Wu, and Xu (manuscript 2026).
  More generally, $t$-site marginals satisfy $O(t\sqrt d/N)$ bosonic and $O(td/N)$ permutation-invariant bounds. Our proof formulates de Finetti approximation as the integrality gap of a symmetric-extension semidefinite program and rounds an optimum by the argmax principle. The sharp bounds have several consequences. For every fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, we construct a channel with input dimension $D=\exp(O_\varepsilon(\sqrt d\log d))=\exp(o(d))$ whose outputs are $\varepsilon$-close to separable states of local dimension $d$ and whose image contains every such separable state, thereby refuting Watrous's disentangler conjecture. We also obtain deterministic $\exp(\widetilde O(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$-time algorithms for explicit Best Separable State without perfect completeness and for trace-distance separability testing.
  Finally, spectral truncation gives the first dimension-free bosonic de Finetti theorem in Hilbert--Schmidt distance, with the optimal rate $Θ(N^{-1/2})$ when the dimension may grow.

</details>


### [89] [Nearly tight lower bounds for estimating quantum functionals: Uhlmann fidelity, trace distance, and von Neumann entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02600)
*Qisheng Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes a unified framework for lower bounds in estimating quantum state functionals, proving several open problems and demonstrating that $	ilde{Ω}(N^2)$ samples are necessary for certain estimations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for definitive lower bounds in quantum state functional estimation to clarify the sample complexity and improve algorithm efficiency in quantum computing.

Method: The authors develop a framework that derives lower bounds for Uhlmann fidelity, trace distance, and von Neumann entropy estimation, using quantum sample-to-query lifting.

Result: They establish lower bounds of $	ilde{Ω}(N^2)$ samples for estimating key functionals, leading to $	ilde{Ω}(N)$ query lower bounds, demonstrating the implications for quantum algorithms developed since 2016.

Conclusion: The findings assert the near-optimality of numerous quantum algorithms, suggesting that the established lower bounds are significant for understanding quantum sample complexity.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a unified framework for proving lower bounds for estimating functionals of quantum states. We therefore resolve several open problems by establishing lower bounds that match known upper bounds: we show that it requires $\widetildeΩ(N^2)$ samples to estimate the Uhlmann fidelity, trace distance, and von Neumann entropy. Moreover, they immediately imply matching query lower bounds of $\widetildeΩ(N)$ by quantum sample-to-query lifting. These lower bounds imply the near-optimality of a dozen quantum algorithms since 2016.

</details>


<div id='hep-ph'></div>

# hep-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [90] [Testing Lepton Wave Function Factorization in $^{71}\text{Ge}$ Electron Capture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00120)
*M. Cadeddu,N. Cargioli,M. Cau,F. Dordei,L. Ferro*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes electron capture rates in $^{71}	ext{Ge}$, emphasizing the role of non-factorization effects and the interplay of nuclear transition density with leptonic wave functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test atomic wave functions and investigate non-factorization impacts on nuclear electron capture rates.

Method: A non-factorized treatment of the transition matrix element is utilized along with phenomenologically constrained transition densities to predict electron capture rates.

Result: Quantified the effects of non-factorization on capture rates and compared theoretical predictions with experimental averages.

Conclusion: Provided a comprehensive assessment of the theoretical and experimental understanding of electron capture ratios in $^{71}	ext{Ge}$.

Abstract: Electron-capture ratios provide precision tests of atomic wave functions and of possible non-factorization effects in nuclear electron capture. By exploiting exact leptonic wave functions, we present a general framework for predicting the electron capture rates of $^{71}\mathrm{Ge}$. Adopting a non-factorized treatment of the transition matrix element, we investigate the interplay between the nuclear transition density, which encodes the nuclear structure contribution, and the leptonic wave functions. Using phenomenologically constrained transition densities, we quantify the impact of non-factorization effects on the capture rates. Finally, we compare our theoretical predictions for the $L/K$, $M/K$ and $M/L$ electron capture ratios with the current experimental world averages, providing an up-to-date assessment of the theoretical and experimental status of $^{71}\mathrm{Ge}$ electron capture.

</details>


### [91] [Low-lying singly heavy baryon states based on the rigorous calculation with the relativized quark model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00142)
*Zhen-Yu Li,Guo-Liang Yu,Zhi-Gang Wang,Jian-Zhong Gu*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This study analyzes low-lying positively-parity singly heavy baryon states using an improved relativized quark model, achieving a statistical error of 6.96 MeV in mass calculations, confirming model reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically study the mass spectra and mixing effects of low-lying singly heavy baryons, enhancing understanding of their structure and interactions.

Method: Utilized an improved relativized quark model and two-step GEM for high-precision mass calculations and analysis of the mixing effects dependent on quark flavor symmetry and baryon parity.

Result: The analysis revealed that the mixing effects vary according to the flavor symmetry of light quarks, and the high-precision calculations matched experimental data with a small statistical error of 6.96 MeV.

Conclusion: The findings confirm the reliability of the calculation scheme and provide valuable data for future experimental and theoretical studies on baryons.

Abstract: In this work, the low-lying $\mathbf{6}_{F}$ singly heavy baryon states with positive-parity are studied in detail in the framework of the relativized quark model by using the improved calculation scheme which has successfully explained the fine structure of the low-lying negative-parity singly heavy baryons. The complete mass spectra of all the singly heavy baryon families obtained in the same framework and calculation scheme are systematically analyzed. The baryon states marked with (mass)$J^{P}$ are obtained by considering the mixing effect rigorously. It is found that the mixing effect in the singly heavy baryons depends on the flavor symmetry of the two light quarks and the baryon parity. The results show that the high-precision calculation can reproduce most of the data perfectly, and the statistical error between the calculated masses and the experimental data is only 6.96 MeV. This confirms the reliability of the improved calculation scheme. The rigorous calculation achieved by the two-step GEM enables us to analyze the detailed behavior of the various strong interaction components within the baryons with a high-precision and discover the truth of the ``soft QCD''. The large amount of data obtained in this work serves as the reliable references for related experimental and theoretical researches.

</details>


### [92] [Extra-dimensional Origins of Chemical Potentials at the Cosmological Collider](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00158)
*Raman Sundrum,Zhaohui Xu*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores particle production mechanisms in inflationary models involving higher-dimensional gauge fields, identifying scenarios for creating heavy particles without Boltzmann suppression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate particle production during inflation using higher-dimensional gauge theories, leveraging concepts like the Schwinger mechanism.

Method: The study analyzes a rolling inflaton field interacting with multiple gauge fields, leading to charged and neutral particle production, including Kaluza-Klein excitations, through minimal gauge interactions.

Result: Particles heavier than the inflationary Hubble scale can be generated, particularly charged Kaluza-Klein states, without Boltzmann suppression, confirming predictions of the model.

Conclusion: Realistic inflationary models can be constructed that align with both theoretical frameworks and experimental evidence, enhancing our understanding of particle dynamics in cosmological settings.

Abstract: We study the realization of the chemical potential mechanism in cosmological collider physics in a robust class of inflationary models arising from multiple higher-dimensional gauge fields. The rolling inflaton background corresponds to an electric field in the extra dimension in which charged particles are produced analogously to the Schwinger mechanism, while neutral particles can be produced through non-minimal interactions. We show that particles heavier than the inflationary Hubble scale can be created without Boltzmann suppression in both cases. In particular, charged Kaluza-Klein excitations can be created through minimal gauge interactions. We construct realistic models along these lines consistent with both theoretical and experimental constraints.

</details>


### [93] [Ubiquitous Corotation of Dark Matter Halos: Implications for Direct Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00161)
*Dylan Folsom,Carlos Blanco,Mariangela Lisanti,Mark Vogelsberger*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This study highlights the non-isotropic nature of dark matter velocity in the Milky Way, affecting direct detection predictions and introducing a correlation with rotational velocity that could reduce uncertainty in measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the halo-to-halo variance in the dark matter phase-space distribution and its implications for direct detection experiments.

Method: Analysis of nearly one hundred Milky Way-like galaxies from the TNG50 simulation to assess the azimuthal velocity of dark matter relative to the baryonic disk's rotation.

Result: The median azimuthal velocity of dark matter is nonzero and corotates with baryonic structures, significantly affecting scattering rates and introducing considerable uncertainty in detection experiments.

Conclusion: Measurements of Milky Way's rotation can substantially reduce uncertainty in dark matter interaction cross-section estimates, emphasizing the importance of galaxy formation history in direct detection studies.

Abstract: Cosmological simulations have recently begun to quantify the halo-to-halo variance in the phase-space distribution of dark matter around the Sun. We use a sample of nearly one hundred Milky Way-like galaxies from the TNG50 simulation to determine what aspects of this variance control the predictions for dark matter direct detection. Contrary to the isotropy assumed in the standard halo model, we find the dark matter median azimuthal velocity is nonzero and preferentially corotating, i.e., in the direction of the baryonic disk's rotation, ranging from 6-70 km/s (16th-84th percentile). This corotation suppresses predicted scattering rates in laboratory experiments searching for dark matter lighter than 50 GeV and significantly affects the expected daily modulation amplitude for directional detectors. In particular, this induces a 21% uncertainty on the upper limit of the dark matter-nucleon interaction cross section at peak sensitivity for a typical isotropic ton-scale experiment. This uncertainty is not irreducible, however: it is strongly correlated with the rotational velocity. If studies of the Milky Way's formation history determine the rotation speed, this astrophysical uncertainty is reduced to 7%.

</details>


### [94] [The flavour of SU(15) composite quarks and leptons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00166)
*Benoît Assi,Ryan Plestid,Amartya Sengupta,Jure Zupan*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores a chiral gauge theory framework where the Standard Model particles are composite bound states, analyzing how flavour dynamics arise and impact observables, predicting significant contributions to flavour-changing processes and rare decays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the emergence of Standard Model particles in a composite framework and their flavour structure within an $SU(15)_p$ gauge theory.
